SAM Volume II | Reading this edition
Brady | 10 November 2026
Reading this edition
The revised edition connects the retained matter and spin constructions to the completed nuclear-source and formation work of
8–10 November 2026. Its central sequence is source, complete energy comparison, preparation protocol and independent physical
response. The 5 November manuscript and its scientiﬁc predecessors are preserved separately.
Part I establishes ﬁnite admission, particle grammar, mass channels, the nuclear ledger and the retained binding benchmark.
Part II develops exact spin sources, hidden memory, composition and the complete physical response receiver. Part III carries
these objects into nuclear, atomic and molecular construction and the common-energy physical map. Binary contact coordinates,
physical particle inventories and electronic spin labels retain their own source deﬁnitions.
Chapter XI now includes the veriﬁed relaxed-He3 complete-payment intervals, the earlier free-reference and shared-ﬁeld co-
variance theorems, the actual He4/H2 receiver, and the veriﬁed source-connected Li6 energy certiﬁcates. The latter use one
explicitly declared scalar Hamiltonian for joined matter and every proper fragment. The selected hopping construction and the
ﬂavor-preserving unchanged preparation are distinct receiving maps.
Chapter XII develops source-compatible arrival, ﬂavor-preserving controls, full-Fock ﬁnite-time ﬁeld loading and its signed
work account. Chapter XVI turns the proposed interaction into independent response tests: legal states with equal incidence
currents, an eleven-state static-kernel design, ﬂavor vertices, dynamic poles and a quantitative calibration tolerance. Appendix
A retains the longer coherent-battery, clock and thermal constructions with their apparatus hypotheses. Appendix B records
veriﬁcation scope and source custody.
The He3, free-reference, covariance and nine-claim Li6 cases retain their internal separate-proof/code veriﬁcation. The newest
arrival, loading, response and reservoir constructions retain their analytical and internal computational evidence without acquiring
a new independent case status. A declared Hamiltonian, a variational energy certiﬁcate, an implementable formation law and a
calibrated isotope prediction remain different stages of the same program. Their precise interfaces are developed in the chapters.
Earlier source laws and ﬁtted or held comparisons keep their original meaning. In particular the historical scalar-current helium
test in Chapter XVI is distinct from the new scalar cavity benchmark. The spin section retains its source Dirichlet, dense-source
and stopped/partial calculation records. No scientiﬁc campaign, binding ﬁt or physical unit assignment was performed for this
editorial revision. The editable edition includes baseline hashes, selected evidence snapshots, a coverage map and reproducible
build instructions.

SAM Volume II | Chapter I
Brady | 10 November 2026
SAM VOLUME II
Matter admission and ﬁnite closure
Chapter I
I.1 The question at the matter boundary
The Substrate Accumulation Model begins with matter as a
source of accumulation. That statement leaves a question on
the source side: what counts as matter, and what kind of closed
object is allowed to supply the source? Volume I develops
the accumulation ﬁeld and its readouts. This volume develops
the ﬁnite objects that enter the matter account, the distinctions
among their source and mass channels, and the ways in which
they return support to the accumulation description.
The central construction is a typed ﬁnite closure. Connec-
tor support supplies relations; carrier routing supplies the traf-
ﬁc needed by the conﬁguration; an admission condition distin-
guishes an eligible closed write from a candidate that does not
close as matter. A numerical value is one ﬁeld of such a con-
struction. It is not a substitute for the construction itself. A
connector and a material row can carry equal numbers while
answering different questions. A carrier can be essential to a
material conﬁguration without becoming another material con-
stituent. A ﬁnite catalog can contain rejected candidates with-
out declaring those candidates to be physical objects.
The matter account therefore has two complementary direc-
tions. In the ﬁrst, a supported conﬁguration is admitted as a
ﬁnite material write. In the second, an admitted write emits
a source-support quantity that is decomposed and compressed
before it enters the accumulation readout. The directions meet
at the admitted object, but they are not inverse formulas. In
particular, a source-support number is evaluated after the can-
didate has passed its declared matter-admission gate.
This distinction is the organizing principle of the volume. It
makes possible a readable account of particle grammar, mass
lift, nuclear ledgers, binding, isotope families and carrier re-
turn without treating every occurrence of the same integer as
the same object. It also permits failed physical identiﬁcations
to remain in the record while preserving exact algebra that sur-
vives them. The direct source-to-rest-mass bridge is an im-
portant example: its failure constrains the interpretation of the
source channel; it does not erase the ﬁnite ledger from which
the channel was constructed.[1, 2]
I.2 What is inherited and what is being asserted
The structural values used here are received from the substrate
framework:
αH = 2,
D = 3,
(I.1)
with surface multiplicity and route radix
S = αD
H = 8,
R = α2
HD = 12.
(I.2)
The resulting domain capacity is R2 = 144. The retained and
carrier quantities will be M = 126 and Θ = 18. Other recurring
values include the full face F = 81 and terminal ledger L =
162.
These equations have different logical roles. Choosing the
structural framework and interpreting its quantities are model
commitments. Once the values and operations are ﬁxed, con-
sequences such as 144/8 = 18 are exact arithmetic. A reported
test may then establish that a particular implementation repro-
duces its speciﬁed source table or survives selected controls.
A comparison against measured masses adds another layer,
whose units, anchors and statistical scope must be stated. None
of these layers should be silently substituted for another.
The distinction matters even when a formula is short. The
equation R = 12 can be a locked input to a downstream test
rather than an independently measured output of that test. The
equation M = 126 can express retained structural capacity
while the element roster retains its own source and observa-
tion status. A row labeled by a familiar particle name can be
the outcome of a reveal stage rather than the object from which
a generator was derived blind. The chapters that follow retain
the order in which the information enters.
This book describes the established construction at those lev-
els. Exact derivations are given with their domains. Computa-
tional results are reported as results of the cited records unless
an inspection is speciﬁcally described. Measured comparisons
remain comparisons. The physical interpretation is developed
where the evidence supports it and bounded where the con-
struction has not supplied the necessary bridge.
I.3 A notation that preserves the source categories
Three uses of the letter A need to be separated at the outset.
Nuclear mass number is
Anuc = Z +N.
(I.3)
The accumulation ﬁeld is
A(r) = rs
r .
(I.4)
The non-row conjugation operator will be written A. Its inputs
are charged antecedents in a declared row schema. It is not
a nuclear count or the radial accumulation ﬁeld. Subscripts
are retained when a passage contains more than one of these
objects.
The letter M also needs care. Here M = 126 is retained ca-
pacity. A row-level readout is written Mobs(r) when following
the source convention. The historical subscript “obs” does not
by itself guarantee that the number is an independently mea-
sured mass. Its meaning is the ﬁnite readout ﬁeld in the source
schema. Where an experimental value is used, it is identiﬁed
separately as the measured comparator and supplied with its
physical units.
The native source, debit or credit, and normalized lift are
denoted
K,
X,
Y = X
K .
(I.5)

The normalized quotient requires K ̸= 0. A source table con-
taining a K = X = 0 carrier does not acquire a deﬁned Y merely
because the row belongs in the table. That endpoint will be re-
tained in the mass chapter rather than silently ﬁlled.
The matter-side source support is qA.
The nuclear rest-
source and coupling-source channels are Qmass and Qsub.
These symbols express a deliberate separation. The volume
does not assume a universal equality among K, X, qA, the two
nuclear channels and a measured mass. Their relationships
must be established by the operators acting on the correspond-
ing types.
A row is therefore more than a displayed value. We use the
source representation
r = (S ,Σ,a,O),
(I.6)
where S identiﬁes the source table, Σ its schema, a its typed
address and O the acting operator. Fields such as charge, sign,
depth, partition, stability, bin and revealed name belong inside
that identity. Two rows with the same native value can have
different schemas, addresses or operators. Conversely, differ-
ent representations of one row can preserve its identity while
changing a derived display ﬁeld.
This notation has practical consequences.
A comparison
must say which ﬁeld is compared. A catalog count must say
which predicate selects its rows. A transformation must say
whether it creates an admitted row, transforms a ﬁeld of an
existing row, or merely supplies an address used by another
operation. Such declarations are part of the mathematical spec-
iﬁcation, not explanatory decoration.
I.4 The operational closure gate
Let a supported conﬁguration be
X = (Γ,T ,A ),
(I.7)
where Γ is connector support, T is carrier routing and A is a
typed address. The matter gate evaluates the conﬁguration:
Gmatter(X ) ∈ {ADMIT,REJECT}.
(I.8)
On an admitted conﬁguration it emits a ﬁnite material write,
m = Wﬁnite(X ).
(I.9)
The notation summarizes the source-deﬁned gate; it does not
replace its class-speciﬁc predicates with a new universal for-
mula. The point of the abstraction is to show what information
the gate receives and what category it may emit.
A native generator acts earlier in the chain. It can assign
a number to a candidate that is subsequently rejected by sta-
bility or matter admission. This is why a generator identity
can hold for a fake closure. The generator and the rejection
predicate are not inconsistent: they answer different questions.
Likewise, a hidden-support row can carry a nonzero structural
value without producing matter-side source support.
This ordering prevents an otherwise easy circular argument.
Suppose a formula can be evaluated on any number.
If a
nonzero output were then taken as sufﬁcient for matter ad-
mission, the formula would be both the candidate construction
and its own validator. In the retained construction the admis-
sion predicate is supplied independently by the row’s declared
source schema. The support formula is used only after that
predicate is satisﬁed. It reports a consequence of admission
rather than creating admission by arithmetic alone.
The
distinction
between
strong
support
and
particle
identity already appears in the reported QP034 selec-
tor.
That run records a stable-lane closure fraction of
0.9999993719460315 and a strongest-slot self-support score
of 0.9880873618629215, but zero ﬁxed-point slot promotions.
These values are reported from the source account, not a fresh
calculation in this volume. Their signiﬁcance is categorical: a
high support score did not automatically become a localized
particle identity.[3]
I.5 Derivation of the released and retained domain
The split begins with the closed radix-square domain,
R2 = 122 = 144.
(I.10)

to the domain gives the released side,

The carrier value is also recovered through the structural ex-

two speciﬁed arithmetic routes.
The interpretation is a complementary split into unlike roles.
The released side is carrier trafﬁc; the retained side is capac-
ity for ﬁnite writes. Nothing in the addition law makes the
carrier an ordinary matter row. Nor does the retained capacity
equation supply all the predicates needed to decide which in-
dividual candidates are admitted. It supplies a ﬁnite structural
account within which those decisions are made.
The decomposition also explains why the numbers recur at
different stages. A construction may use 18 as a carrier quan-
tity, a coordinate may evaluate to 18, and a debit may have
magnitude 18. Those occurrences can be related by a speciﬁc
operator or a tested crosswalk, but the shared numeral is not
the crosswalk. This rule becomes especially important when
the matter account reaches nuclear coordinates and informa-
tional geometry.

source through this gate.
I.7 The null witness and the cost of ignoring type
The LC06 replay retains the null-conjugate witness QP093A-
0088. In its source account the native value is 18 and the debit
is 18, leaving
Mobs = 0,
qA = 0.
(I.24)
The cancellation occurs before a nonzero material readout
could be emitted. The row is particularly informative because
the native value equals the carrier number Θ. Neither the equal-
ity nor the presence of the row in a catalog licenses a matter
promotion.
If the row were counted as material simply because its na-
tive value is nonzero, the resulting inventory would lose the
distinction between candidate and admitted object. If it were
treated as a carrier simply because the number is 18, its actual
source role would also be overwritten. The null witness there-
fore tests a stronger discipline than numerical consistency: the
complete row must be interpreted with its native value, debit,
admission status and output together.
The reported CR238 matter-gate regrade applies the same
discipline across several source classes. Its account reports
reconstruction of 126 admitted matter rows, 13 blocked rows
with zero material readout and support, and eight hidden-
support rows generated by
p+ p2
R2 .
(I.25)
These class totals are scoped to that source test. They should
not be added or identiﬁed with every other inventory in the
book. Their purpose is to demonstrate that nonzero hidden
structure and a blocked matter channel can coexist within a
typed construction.[3]
I.8 Connector roles and the substrate atom
The connector and partition alphabet is
P = {1,2,3,4,6,8,9,12}.
(I.26)
The substrate-atom packet is expressed by
X1+W8 = W9.
(I.27)
In this expression X1 is a transient activator or axis-fee role,
W8 is the octahedral relational substrate object, and W9 is their
completed relation. The equation must be read operationally.
W9 is not an extra ordinary material constituent added along-
side X1 and W8. It identiﬁes completion of the relation repre-
sented by the packet.
The longer ordered expression
1,1,2,3,4,6,8,8,9,9,12,18,81
(I.28)
contains heterogeneous roles. The ﬁrst twelve terms sum to
81, and appending the ﬁnal 81 gives 162. These equalities are
exact. Their interpretation requires the role labels: activator,
substrate and completion roles are not identical to the mass-
lift values; Θ18 is a carrier/overlap role; F81 is an aggregate
full face. The separate A-ﬁeld carrier is transport infrastructure
outside this list.
This distinction repairs an overly uniform reading of the ex-
pression as thirteen homogeneous tensors. The list has thirteen
positions, but a position count does not erase the differences
among its entries. An implementation receiving the expres-
sion must preserve those differences, or it can create a false
7

correspondence merely by pairing equal values. Later chapters
use the current typed reading when discussing carrier reception
and the export to computation.[5]
The grammar work of September 2026 made the two roles
of Θ18 explicit.
In the cross-examination of the 321-row
source join, the ordinary values p ∈ P have the lift law L(p) =
p+ p2/144, and “Theta18 is the explicit ninth exception: L =
18, zero direct lift or fee, with connector and carrier roles.” The
generative return built on it uses eight support blocks and both
Θ roles, with Hmin(P) = (P mod 18)/144 whose ⌊P/18⌋ + 1
minima distribute the Θ roles and Hmax(P) = ⌊P/12⌋+ fr/144
with an exact Bellman certiﬁcate; the nine-value grammar gen-
erates 15,484,855 typed constructions at partition 144.[156]
Two boundaries were recorded with it. The owner-directed
residual follow-up that joined the B5T tensors, the B4 Θ co-
ordinates and the Bethe-block incidence found that “Theta ad-
dress coordinates are not connector counts,” all 184 outcomes
descriptive; and the binding representation of Chapter V, B5T,
assigns only the eight ordinary values to its pointed roles and
never asked whether Θ belongs among them. So Θ18 is a con-
nector and a carrier in the grammar, and the type rule of Chap-
ter III still holds: the shared numeral is not the crosswalk, and
whether the ninth value enters a binding locator is a change of
target construction that no campaign has yet run.
I.9 A failure that ﬁxes the source interpretation
The direct bridge tested in CR239 proposed that one scale
could identify a native source quantity with measured inertial
and gravitational mass:
mi(P) = mg(P) = µQQ(P).
(I.29)
The carbon-12 anchor was arranged so that
Q(12C) = 7117
16 ,
µQ = 192
7117 u
(I.30)
per source unit, giving exactly 12u at the anchor. An exact
anchor is necessary to specify the conversion, but it cannot
establish how the conversion behaves away from the anchor.
The reported non-anchor result fails the locked compari-
son. The direct bridge has RMS residual 0.189480, while the
mass-number control has RMS 0.00202. The source result
records a failed direct bridge. Its isotope increment is also too
small: the proposed increment µQ/8 = 24/7117u is approxi-
mately 0.003372u, and the reported measured-to-predicted ra-
tios across fourteen isotope pairs have mean 296.45 and me-
dian 296.66.[3]
The residual is structured. The source account reports RMS
0.0023 for twelve balanced N = Z rows, compared with 0.2152
for thirty-seven rows with neutron excess, and Spearman cor-
relation +0.984 with N −Z. The native quantity still contains
information: its shufﬂed control is worse. But information in a
source variable is weaker than the claimed direct identiﬁcation
with measured mass.
The correction preserves the coupling channel and separates
the rest-source channel. This is the starting point of the nuclear
and binding chapters. The ﬁnite ledger remains useful because
the failure identiﬁes which quantity was being asked to do too
much. A different ﬁeld is not introduced merely to conceal a
bad residual; the record retains the rejected identity, its anchor,
its control and the neutron-excess structure that motivates the
separation.
I.10 What the ﬁrst closure supplies
Finite matter enters through typed closure. Its admitted sup-
port splits into complementary carrier and retained channels,
while operators, hidden support and matter rows keep their dis-
tinct roles. The failed direct source-as-mass bridge supplies
the reason to retain separate coupling and rest-source chan-
nels. The next chapters develop the ﬁnite particle surfaces and
nuclear ledger; the later source chapters construct their condi-
tional states, contact histories, occupied atoms and molecular
recipients.

SAM VOLUME II
Typed Particle Grammar and Finite Surfaces
Chapter II
II.1 What a particle row contains
The transition from a ﬁnite closure to a particle catalog re-
quires more than a list of integers. A partition value supplies
an address component; it does not by itself say whether the ad-
dressed object is a single write, a composite, a carrier, a hidden
support packet, or a rejected closure. The grammar developed
here gives those distinctions an explicit place. Its central object
is a typed row whose identity includes the source table, the row
schema, the address and the operator that acts on that address.
A conventional particle name, when available, is attached later.
This ordering makes the ﬁnite construction recoverable even
where a familiar name is absent.
A useful abstract record is
r = (source,schema,id,class,address,
bin,status,operator,outputs).
(II.1)
The address can contain a partition tuple, sign or charge coor-
dinate, radix depth, route combination and surface ﬁelds. Not
every schema uses every ﬁeld. In particular, a sign ﬁeld at-
tached to a debit operator cannot automatically be substituted
for an electric charge or for a matter–antimatter status. The
output record should likewise keep native value, source sup-
port, debit, admission and external comparison distinct. This
is an editorial expression of the source’s typing requirements,
not a claim that one universal row format already implements
all physical sectors.
The adopted structural inputs remain D = 3, αH = 2, R = 12
and S = 8. They enter exact conditional formulas below. The
formulas are exact within their declared domains; the choice
of domain and the physical interpretation of the outputs are
additional statements. Thus the manuscript can preserve a suc-
cessful algebraic compression without presenting it as an inde-
pendently demonstrated ontology of all particles.
The principal sources for this chapter are SAMA-D000026,
Typed Matter Surfaces and Row Grammar, and SAMA-
D000027, Finite Particle Grammar and 321-Row Census, as
assembled in the unapproved review rendering; the current in-
tegrated manuscript supplies the surrounding authority map.1
[1, 2, 3] The distinctions in this chapter are necessary for read-
ing the mass formulas in Chapter III and the nuclear construc-
tions that follow.

II.2 A family of ﬁnite surfaces
There is no single unqualiﬁed “number of SAM particles” that
can replace all source counts. The compact mass closure con-
tains 35 rows. Its purpose is to recover the compact predic-
tion chain. The full ﬁnite catalog contains 321 rows and in-
cludes candidates, bound objects, carriers, hidden support and
rejected closures. The normalized-lift table contains 139 rows,
of which 138 have a deﬁned quotient. The matter inventory
contains 126 rows, divided into 63 stable single writes and 63
bound composites. The tensor-compatible stable surface con-
tains 80 rows. The periodic construction contains 126 element-
family rows. Finally, the N100 computation basis contains 100
typed rows after its declared pruning. These surfaces answer
different questions.
For a source catalog C and a source-deﬁned predicate P, one
may write
SP = {r ∈ C : P(r) = 1}.
(II.2)
Other relations between source universes and surfaces are pro-
jections rather than simple subset selections. In either case, the
predicate or projection is part of the deﬁnition. Equality of car-
dinalities does not imply equality of members, and equality of
members would not, without a schema map, imply equality of
interpretation. Two separate surfaces can each have 126 rows
while one organizes admitted writes and the other organizes
element families.
This distinction resolves an otherwise tempting confusion
with the retained capacity M = 126. The arithmetic M = 7R2/8
is a structural capacity statement. A 126-row inventory is a
census under a particular gate. A 126-row periodic table is a
family-indexed construction. The recurring integer may moti-
vate a proposed structural relation, but the relation has to be
supplied rather than inferred from the repeated numeral. The
same applies to the 80-row surface and the 81-place full face:
one cannot turn an operator into an extra particle merely to
make the totals coincide.
The 139-row lift table provides a particularly useful exam-
ple. Its source construction comprises 126 promoted rows plus
13 carrier-tensor rows. It is neither a new census of 139 ma-
terial particles nor an inconsistent version of the 126-row in-
ventory. Its question is which normalized lift operator applies
to each supplied row. One row has both zero source and zero
debit, so the table contains an endpoint that cannot be evalu-
ated as a quotient. Retaining that row is necessary to preserve
the schema; excluding it from quotient arithmetic is necessary
to preserve mathematics.
The older CR119 reveal records 321 particle-catalog rows,
126 matter rows,
126 periodic rows and 573 identity-
assignment rows. Those numbers already belonged to separate
schemas. CR252 subsequently establishes the current 321-row
reconstruction baseline; it does not erase the older reveal or
9

SAM Volume II | Chapter II
Brady | 10 November 2026
allow its counts to be recombined into a new physical census.
A later computation basis likewise selects a representation for
computation without rewriting the source catalog from which
it was selected.
II.3 The current 321-row reconstruction
CR252 records a deterministic catalog refresh. Its seven bins
have the exact distribution
321 = 63+25+42+169+6+8+8,
= stable+resonance+antimatter

(II.3)
The words in the second expression stand for the correspond-
ing counts in the ﬁrst.
In full, there are 63 stable-matter
rows, 25 unstable-resonance rows, 42 antimatter-conjugate
rows, 169 bound-composite rows, six carrier-only rows, eight
hidden-source-support rows and eight rejected-fake-closure
rows. These are the catalog’s organizational bins, not seven
lists of experimentally identiﬁed species.
The inspected CR252 summary reports 321 baseline rows,
321 regenerated rows, stable candidate identiﬁers, complete
classiﬁcation and 321 unchanged rows present in both ver-
sions. Baseline, regenerated output and the second-run wrong-
control receipt share the SHA-256 recorded in the source man-
ifest. The reconstruction retains six carriers with zero promo-
tions. It also records 286 rows satisfying its broader matter-
allowed predicate. That ﬁeld is not a sum to be guessed from
the English bin labels: it is a separately evaluated predicate in
the 321-row schema. Its value cannot replace either the 126-
row inventory or the 80-row promoter.
The available runner makes the meaning of this result con-
crete. It snapshots the existing QP093A catalog, invokes the
QP093A generator, and compares the regenerated output with
the baseline. This is evidence of deterministic reconstruction
of an existing artifact. It is not a second, independent discov-
ery of the catalog or an empirical observation of all its mem-
bers. The present manuscript uses the inspected receipts with-
out claiming a new execution of the generator.2
[8] The amendment is part of the result. The original V-3/F2
speciﬁcation produced a failure; a precommit amendment cor-
rected the criterion, while preserving that original failure in the
audit trail. A readable account therefore says that the amended
reconstruction passes, rather than silently presenting the ﬁnal
PASS as the only event. The amended test’s useful content is
the unchanged artifact and its correctly typed comparison. The
history of a speciﬁcation correction does not weaken a valid
identity check, but neither should an identity check erase its
own provenance.
One analytical wrong control changes R. A triadic (2,8,8)
value changes from 4752 at R = 12 to 3960 at R = 10, a dif-
ference of 792. A pair (3,6) changes from 225 to 189, a dif-
ference of 36. These checks show that the adopted radix is
load-bearing in those formulas. They do not establish that no

II.4 Generation, admission and reveal
The operational sequence is
typed address −→ native generator
−→ stability classiﬁcation
−→ matter admission
−→ label or comparator reveal.
(II.4)
The stages cannot be compressed into the assertion that a gener-
ated number is an observed particle. A generator can evaluate a
rejected closure. A stable candidate can remain unnamed. An
admitted source can possess a nonzero structural value without
that value being a measured rest mass. A known label attached
downstream does not become a permissible input to an earlier
target-barred construction.
This ordering also explains why rejected objects belong in
the evidence record. If every row that matched a native for-
mula were automatically admitted, the formula would have no
independent stability content. The three-body extension below
supplies a direct counterexample: thirteen rejected source rows
match the same generator as stable candidates. They preserve
the distinction between structural evaluation and the later gate.
Removing them from the presentation would make the success-
ful generator appear stronger than it is.
The identity should remain reversible before an authorized
conventional name is attached. A SAM placeholder can iden-
tify the source row without implying that an experimental iden-
tiﬁcation exists. When a source does authorize contact with
a conventional name, that contact is retained with its prove-
nance. Neither inventing familiar names nor refusing all famil-
iar names serves the construction; the appropriate practice is
to carry the exact naming authority with the row.
II.5 Two-body bound-color grammar
For a source-classiﬁed bound-color pair with partition (a,b),
the detailed CR128 law is
M2(a,b) = Rab+D|a−b|.
(II.5)
Both terms are symmetric under exchange. Hence M2(a,b) =
M2(b,a), even where the catalog retains two orderings because
a later signed debit distinguishes them. The generator’s ex-
change symmetry is not permission to merge all downstream
ﬁelds.
For (a,b) = (1,2), the product contribution is 12·1·2 = 24
and the difference contribution is 3|1−2| = 3, giving M2 = 27.
For (3,3), the difference vanishes and M2 = 108. For (3,6),
M2 = 216 + 9 = 225, agreeing with the CR252 radix control.
These examples preserve the scale factors rather than only the
shape of the formula.
A source reconciliation is important here. The integrated
overview abbreviates the two-body shape as product plus ab-
solute difference, displaying ab+|a−b|. The focused CR128

account explicitly locks equation (II.5), gives its weighted ex-
amples and reports its match counts. This chapter uses that de-
tailed numerical law; the unweighted overview is not treated
as an equivalent implementation.3
The recorded scope is 36 rows: 30 asymmetric and six sym-
metric pairs, with no parse or partition-algebra violations. The
formula was recovered from this catalog, so 36/36 is an in-
sample consistency result. The sealed future-row rule ﬁxes a
possible falsiﬁer for a newly eligible pair. That seal speciﬁes
how a later case could test the law; it does not retroactively
make the discovery sample blind. CR128 also leaves the asym-
metric doublet’s debit and the symmetric pair’s observed-mass
offset to separate operators. The native pair law has a deﬁnite
accomplishment and a deﬁnite domain.
II.6 Three-body symmetry and its extension
For an OCTET-composite triple, CR129 records
M3(a,b,c) = RD(a2 +b2 +c2) = 36(a2 +b2 +c2).
(II.6)
Each coordinate enters through its square. Consequently ev-
ery permutation of a triple gives the same native value. The
worked triple (1,2,3) has squared sum 14 and native value
504. The source reports 76/76 three-body OCTET matches,
while routing 28 two-body OCTET rows through their distinct
two-body law, with 28/28 matches in that route.
CR129c extends the same three-body generator to 44
ground-baryon rows: fourteen closed stable candidates, sev-
enteen closed heavy candidates and thirteen rejected fake clo-
sures. All 44 match. Together with the OCTET set, this gives
120 reported three-body matches. This number is a scoped test
total across these classes, not another replacement census for
the 321-row catalog. In particular, the thirteen rejected rows in
the extension and the eight rows in the full catalog’s rejected
bin are counts from different reported class/status selections
and must not be silently equated.
The symmetric structure has a transparent algebraic rewrite.
Put

∆2 = (a−b)2 +(b−c)2 +(a−c)2.
(II.7)

II.7 The three-body debit and its failed precursors
The three-body native value is not its surface debit. CR129b
retains a sequence of failed proposals that locates the eventual
operator. Summing pairwise two-body debits gave the wrong
sign and discrepancies by factors of roughly seven to ﬁfteen.

global (9/8)M/R3 surcharge overshot uniformly by 18/17. A
charge-slot correction then exposed a residual linear in the ab-
solute charge coordinate. The successful neutral construction
applies the 9/8 weighting only to the middle slot of a 1:2:1
decomposition.

II.8 One-body ladders, carriers and hidden support
CR131 assigns the V4_1 single-write family to a fermion-half-

Mnative = qabsRdK(qsign,status).
(II.13)

at d = 0,1,2; negative matter has 3/2,18,216. Each step mul-

cient rule failed on the 42 antimatter rows. Including the status-
dependent swap gives the recorded 90/90 closure, comprising
48 matter and 42 antimatter rows. These are generator-class
counts, not the later 80-row promoted surface.

II.9 How to compare rows without losing the grammar
Three elementary comparisons summarize the practical use of
the grammar. First, compare two differently ordered bound
pairs.
Their native values agree because both the product
and the absolute difference are exchange-symmetric.
Their
unequal-pair lift can change sign because that operator uses
sgn(a − b). Thus equality in the native column is compatible
with inequality in the signed-debit column. The combination is
not an inconsistency: it records two different questions about
the same ordered address. An implementation that dedupli-
cated rows solely by native value would destroy the informa-
tion needed for the second question.
Second, compare a stable candidate and a rejected three-
body closure. Both can satisfy M3 = 36∑a2
i exactly. Their
difference lies in the later stability and admission ﬁelds. A
generator-only export would make those rows look equally ad-
missible, while an admission-only export would conceal the
useful fact that the formula crosses the stability boundary. The
complete record needs both. This is why retaining rejections is
scientiﬁcally productive rather than merely archival: it identi-
ﬁes precisely which operation has, and which operation lacks,
discriminatory power.
Third, compare a hidden support packet with an admitted
charged matter row. Both can contain the factor p(1+ p/144).
In the hidden packet this is a native structural value and the
matter source remains zero. In the compact charged row the
factor appears inside an admitted qA law with a sign-dependent
coefﬁcient and a depth multiplier. An algebra system may le-
gitimately factor the same polynomial in both expressions; a
physical interpretation may not replace one row type with the
other. Symbolic identity of a factor is a relation between for-
mulas, not an admission rule.
These examples suggest a minimal comparison contract. A
comparison speciﬁes the source versions, the row identiﬁers
or reversible address maps, the columns being compared, the
units or native conventions, and the operator domains.
An
equality claim then says whether it concerns exact rational val-
ues, serialized bytes, class membership or agreement with an
external comparator. Those four senses of agreement are use-
ful but distinct. CR252’s bit identity is stronger than approx-
imate numerical agreement about its serialized artifact, yet it
says nothing by itself about how closely every artifact ﬁeld de-
scribes nature. Conversely, an experimental comparison need
not be bit-identical to a structural table to be informative.
The same discipline clariﬁes the term “zero free parameters.”
Once an in-sample formula has been selected and frozen, it can
have no adjustable coefﬁcients when evaluated on that table.
That fact does not erase the earlier opportunity to choose the
formula after seeing the table. A forward-blind seal constrains
future use, but only a subsequently eligible case can supply
the future test. The manuscript therefore preserves the exact
formulas, their ﬁnite match counts and the chronology of se-
lection together. This offers a more useful account than either
dismissing all ﬁnite exactness or presenting every exact match
as a ﬁrst-principles prediction.
The catalog consequently serves two roles at once. It is
a ﬁnite mathematical object whose arithmetic and reconstruc-
12

SAM Volume II | Chapter II
Brady | 10 November 2026
tion can be examined precisely, and it is a structured proposal
for contact with physical matter. The ﬁrst role supplies repro-
ducibility and explicit falsiﬁers. The second requires the autho-
rized reveal, stability interpretation and empirical comparison
appropriate to each row. Keeping those roles visible lets the
following mass chapter use the grammar without borrowing
evidentiary strength from a different kind of success.
II.10 The 80-row promoter and the ﬁnite export
CR253 selects a tensor-compatible stable surface using the
sufﬁcient predicate:
membership in the stable-matter or
antimatter-conjugate bins, together with hT ∈ {0,1}. The re-
sulting decomposition is
80 = 48 matter+32 antimatter
= 64 charged+16 neutral.
(II.17)
The preregistered bigrade and IT = 0 conditions are redundant
after those ﬁlters. Consequently the source retains a BOUND-

upstream. Exact membership and that control boundary can
both be true. The latter should not be removed merely because
the count matches the intended eighty.
CR280 clariﬁes the semantic type as a tensor-compatible sta-
ble matter surface whose rows are structurally stable, not auto-
matically experimentally identiﬁed particles. This gives the
compact laws a precise domain. It also prevents the non-row
A-conjugation operator from being counted as an eighty-ﬁrst
particle. The relation to a full F81 face is a separate geometric
question, taken up in the carrier-return chapter. Neither the car-
dinality nor the existence of a conjugation transform supplies
an elementwise face map.
The ﬁnite export contains a recoverable catalog, purpose-
indexed surfaces, class-speciﬁc native operators, explicit ad-
mission and reveal stages, exact ﬁnite matches and preserved
controls. Volume III receives these objects with their row iden-
tity, predicates, units, null endpoints and source provenance
intact.

SAM VOLUME II
Mass Channels, Lift Operators, Higgs and Neutrino Packets
Chapter III
III.1 The question a mass formula answers
A formula containing a particle label and a number is not yet a
mass prediction. It may compute a native structural value, an
admitted source contribution, a debit on a surface, a normal-
ized lift, or a downstream readout expressed in physical units.
These quantities can be related, but their relations have to be
stated. The particle grammar supplies the row and selects the
operator; this chapter follows the distinct channels through that
selection. The central lesson of the source record is construc-
tive: retaining the type of a quantity turns several apparently
irregular numerical patterns into exact formulas, while also ex-
posing a direct source-to-mass identiﬁcation that fails.
Write Mnative for the native generator output, qA for admitted
matter-source support, X for a speciﬁed surface debit or credit,
and K for the native source used by a lift-table row. The di-
mensionless quotient Y = X/K, when deﬁned, is a ﬁfth object.
A measured comparator is another ﬁeld. In a nuclear aggrega-
tion the symbols Qsub and Qmass distinguish coupling-source
and rest-source constructions. None of these distinctions can
be removed simply because a particular anchor makes two nu-
merical values coincide.
The source symbol Mobs is likewise a readout ﬁeld whose
meaning must be obtained from its schema. Its subscript alone
is not proof that its value is an experimentally measured mass.
A row’s source provenance and physical conversion map de-
termine whether the number is native, converted, compared or
measured. This matters especially when moving between the
compact base 35-row chain, the 139-row lift table and the 80-
row tensor-compatible surface. They share some conceptual
machinery but do not share an unqualiﬁed numerical column.
The detailed source accounts are SAMA-D000028, Mass,
Lift, Debit and Particle Readout, and SAMA-D000029, Higgs
and Neutrino Packets. The technical spine preserves the same
channel split and the decisive failure-to-correction sequence.[1,
2, 3] The presentation below derives the arithmetic that can be
checked directly, identiﬁes results reported by existing tests,
and retains the external anchors on which physical-unit com-
parisons depend. It does not rerun those tests or supply an
unstated universal conversion from structural number to mea-
sured mass.
III.2 Normalization and the undeﬁned endpoint
For a typed lift row with K ̸= 0, deﬁne
Y = X
K ,
X = KY.
(III.1)
Normalization asks what fraction of the native source is rep-
resented by the declared debit or credit. It can reveal exact
structure obscured by widely varying native magnitudes. The
operation is legitimate only after both the row’s source con-
vention and its operator class have been ﬁxed. Dividing ar-
bitrary columns with similar names would not reproduce the
same mathematical question.
One photon-road carrier in the source table has K = 0 and
X = 0. Its quotient is undeﬁned. The equation 0 = 0·Y would
hold for any ﬁnite Y, so it cannot determine a unique lift value.
Assigning zero would confuse absence of debit with a deﬁned
zero fraction; assigning one would confuse this endpoint with
the fully supported no-depth class. A limiting argument would
require an explicitly speciﬁed family approaching the row, and
no such limit is part of this table’s deﬁnition.
The correct statement is therefore 139 rows and 138 evalu-
able quotients. The carrier remains a valid source row. Only
the quotient ﬁeld is inapplicable. In a computational imple-
mentation, a tagged undeﬁned value preserves more informa-
tion than a numerical zero, because it records the reason nor-
malization was not performed. Summaries must count the row
once while excluding it from statistics or identities that require
a deﬁned Y. This is an elementary mathematical restriction
with an important scientiﬁc effect: the exact closure does not
depend on manufacturing a value for 0/0.
CR243 resolves 96 evaluable rows into ﬁve clean classes.
CR244 resolves the remaining 42 into two unequal-pair classes.
Their union gives
96+42 = 138,
(III.2)
and adding the retained undeﬁned endpoint restores the full ta-
ble count of 139. These counts, rather than a blanket statement
that all 139 quotients close, express the actual ﬁnite comple-
tion.

chapter preserves that positive result without claiming that a
ﬁnite table alone establishes the same operator for every future
physical system.
III.4 Unequal pairs and a load-bearing class trigger
The 42 rows left at CR243’s boundary do not require an arbi-
trary residual correction. CR244 distinguishes thirty ordinary
unequal pairs from twelve OCTET-involved unequal pairs. For
an ordered unequal pair (a,b), the ordinary law is
Yunequal,ordinary = sgn(a−b)|a−b|+D
R4

(III.7)
The OCTET-involved law is
Yunequal,OCTET = sgn(a−b)|a−b|+D2/R
R4
.
(III.8)
For (a,b) = (1,2), the ﬁrst gives −4/20736 = −1/5184. The
second gives −(7/4)/20736 = −7/82944. Their difference at
the same address is not noise: the class trigger changes the
operator. Reversing the ordered pair changes the sign while
preserving the magnitude within the same class.
The recorded result is 30/30 ordinary and 12/12 OCTET-
involved matches. The controls show why both the exponent
and the trigger must be retained. Replacing R4 by R3 reduces
both exact-match counts to zero. Swapping the class trigger
reduces the ordinary match count from thirty to four and the
OCTET match count from twelve to zero. The surviving four
ordinary matches in the wrong-control route do not vindicate
that route; they demonstrate why one must report complete
class counts rather than selected successful examples.
This correction has a useful methodological interpretation.
The initial unresolved set was preserved, rather than concealed
within an overall average. Its internal classiﬁcation then sup-
plied two exact laws. That is evidence that typing adds explana-
tory compression on this source table. It is not evidence that
any difﬁcult data set can always be made exact by adding la-
bels. Here the labels, formulas, counts and failed substitutions
are all part of a ﬁnite record that can be inspected.6

independent measurement of a coupling. The sixteen compact
neutral rows also must not be confused with the twenty-four
neutral generator rows across three depths in CR133; the sur-
face selection differs.
These formulas make a further distinction visible.
The
bare charged native ladder was qabsRdK, while the compact
matter-source law includes the additional connector response
1 + p/R2. Treating both as one formula called “mass” would
obscure which stage supplied that factor. Keeping the source
channel explicit allows their relation to be discussed without
either erasing the earlier ladder or claiming that the new factor
is a direct measured rest-mass correction.

III.13 What the mass sector hands forward
The mass sector now has several ﬁnite results that can be used
without merging their evidentiary roles. The lift table is com-
plete as a typed table: 139 rows, seven evaluable classes cover-
ing 138 quotients, and one explicitly undeﬁned endpoint. The
compact admitted surface has charged and neutral source laws,
together with a non-row conjugation transform and an exact
hard zero. The nuclear branch has a preserved failure of di-
rect source-to-mass conversion and a corrected two-channel
construction whose uniqueness test did not pass. The Higgs
record contains two distinct packets with different operations
and chronology. The neutrino record separates an exact ratio
from its measured scale and retains its non-anchored residual.
These results provide the next chapters with a disciplined
interface. A nuclear ledger may aggregate the corrected chan-
nels, but must not identify them merely because balanced mat-
ter makes them equal. A binding readout may use the channel
gap, but must supply its own nonlinear operation and energy
conversion. A computation may serialize exact rational op-
erators, but must preserve their class triggers and undeﬁned
endpoints. A physical comparison may open measured targets,
but must keep them separate from the inputs of a target-barred
construction.
The beneﬁt of this separation is not only caution. It is also
economy. One does not need to solve mixing, resolve candi-
date uniqueness, or launch a new ﬁtting campaign in order to
preserve an exact ﬁnite lift table. Conversely, a well-preserved
ﬁnite table does not solve those other problems by implica-
tion. The source record can therefore be complete as a record
of what was constructed and tested, while the larger physical
interpretation remains bounded by the evidence actually avail-
able.

SAM VOLUME II
The Nuclear Ledger: Constituents, Channels, and Isotope Structure
Chapter IV
A nucleus brings together two questions that are easy to con-
fuse. What constituents have been counted, and what does
their combination do? The ﬁrst question admits a ﬁnite ac-
counting answer. The second requires a readout of the bound
conﬁguration. This chapter develops the accounting answer
in enough detail that its exact content can be inspected inde-
pendently of any later energy ﬁt. Chapter V then follows the
additional steps needed to compare nuclear readouts with mea-
surements.
The construction has four slots, six aggregate channels, and
a restricted population domain. Its value is not that six equa-
tions replace nuclear dynamics. Its value is that source support,
rest-source bookkeeping, and constituent counts can be carried
together without being silently identiﬁed. This resolves an am-
biguity inherited from the particle problem: a source quantity
can participate in matter construction without already being a
measured rest mass. Nuclear aggregation makes that distinc-
tion particularly visible because balanced and excess neutrons
have the same constituent-count entries but different coupling-
source assignments.
The primary manuscript statement is SAMA-D000030, pre-
served in Section 7 of the frozen Matter and Finite Closure
manuscript.
The technical spine, Sections IV.1–IV.3, sup-
plies the CR247–CR248–CR278 consolidation history and the
failed control discussed below. These are the sources for the
construction.
The derivations in this chapter expand their
stated equations; they do not introduce a newly ﬁtted nuclear
model.[1, 2, 3]
IV.1 The domain and the meaning of a slot
Let Z denote the proton count and N the neutron count. Deﬁne
the nuclear mass number by
A = Z +N.
(IV.1)
Here A is an integer count, not the accumulation ﬁeld or the
conjugation operator bearing the same letter elsewhere in the
framework. A count has no energy unit. It also contains no
assertion about lifetime, stability, or experimental observation.
Those properties require other columns and other evidence.
The stated four-slot decomposition applies to
Z ≥ 0,
N ≥ Z,
(IV.2)
with integer populations. It allocates one balanced neutron per
proton and assigns any remaining neutrons to the excess slot.
The neutral-system electron bookkeeping contributes one elec-
tron per proton. In the order proton, balanced neutron, excess
neutron, electron, the population vector is
n = (Z,Z,N −Z,Z).
(IV.3)
The four entries therefore count different roles. Their sum, N +
2Z, is not the nuclear mass number, because it includes the
electron count. The nucleon populations alone sum to Z +N =
A.
An excess-neutron population cannot be negative. Conse-
quently, substituting a proton-rich isotope into the formulas
may produce a formal algebraic expression, but it does not pro-
duce an admissible four-population construction under Equa-
tion (IV.2). This matters for the familiar light examples: ordi-
nary hydrogen and helium-3 cannot be used as literal demon-
strations of this population basis. Deuterium, tritium, helium-
4, and neutron-rich nuclei can. A larger treatment may adopt
another basis for the excluded domain, but the present deriva-
tion does not supply one by changing a minus sign.
The electron slot makes this a neutral atomic bookkeeping
extension of a nuclear core. It does not locate electrons inside
the nucleus. Nor does the slot’s zero in the nuclear rest-source
column mean that an electron has zero physical rest mass. The
column is deﬁned for the nuclear aggregate being constructed.
The electron count is retained so that charge balance and the
neutral readout convention remain visible; a complete atomic
mass comparison must separately respect electron mass and
electronic binding conventions.
Finally, “balanced” and “excess” designate positions in a
source-accounting basis. The construction does not establish
two experimentally distinguishable neutron species. A basis
may separate contributions that are equal in some observables
and unequal in another channel. Such a separation is useful
precisely when it is kept distinct from a claim about new parti-
cles.
IV.2 The four vectors
Each slot carries a six-component vector in the order
ϕ = (u,d,e,Qmass,Qsub,∆Q).
(IV.4)
The ﬁrst three entries count the stated constituent channels.
The next two are the rest-source and coupling-source channels.
The ﬁnal entry is their signed difference. The inherited coefﬁ-
cient is
κ = 7117
768 .
(IV.5)
Its appearance here is an input from the preceding construction,
not a new determination from nuclear data. With this input, the
four vectors are
ϕp = (2,1,0,4κ,8κ,−4κ),
(IV.6)
ϕnb = (1,2,0,4κ,0,4κ),
(IV.7)
ϕne =
�
1,2,0,4κ, 1
8, 7093
192
�
,
(IV.8)
ϕe = (0,0,1,0,0,0).
(IV.9)
Subscripts nb and ne distinguish balanced and excess neutron
slots; the unscripted electron label e refers to the separate
19

SAM Volume II | Chapter IV
Brady | 10 November 2026
electron slot. Maintaining that distinction in a table header
avoids the misleading impression that the last two slots are in-
terchangeable.
Two features deserve immediate attention.
Both neutron
vectors carry one up-type and two down-type constituents, and
both carry 4κ in the rest-source channel. Their difference lies
entirely in coupling-source and gap accounting. The proton,
by contrast, carries 8κ of coupling source against 4κ of rest
source, so its gap is negative. A negative gap is not a negative
particle mass. It is the difference between two separately typed
positive-channel assignments.
The excess gap follows directly from the two source entries:
4κ − 1
8 = 7117
192 − 24
192
= 7093
192 .
(IV.10)
Thus the last entry of Equation (IV.8) is not an independent
adjustable coefﬁcient. It is constrained by the fourth and ﬁfth
entries. The same relation holds in every slot:
∆Q = Qmass −Qsub.
(IV.11)
A table satisfying the other entries but violating this relation
would be internally inconsistent before any comparison with
experiment.
The six columns should therefore not be mistaken for six
independent degrees of freedom.
Once the population pair
(Z,N), the slot deﬁnitions, and κ have been ﬁxed, the ag-
gregate is ﬁxed. This redundancy is deliberate. It supplies
independent-looking routes through the same bookkeeping, al-
lowing transcription and implementation errors to be caught
without reﬁtting anything.
IV.3 Aggregation and the six identities
The aggregate vector is deﬁned by a linear sum of typed con-
tributions:
Φ(Z,N) = Zϕp +Zϕnb +(N −Z)ϕne +Zϕe.
(IV.12)
Linearity here is an accounting property. It states how the en-
tries are accumulated, not how the bound state’s energy de-
pends on them. That distinction will become essential when
the ledger is passed to the binding layer.
For the up-type count, the proton contributes 2Z, the bal-
anced neutrons contribute Z, and the excess neutrons con-
tribute N −Z. Therefore
u = 2Z +Z +(N −Z)
= 2Z +N.
(IV.13)
Similarly,
d = Z +2Z +2(N −Z)
= Z +2N,
(IV.14)
e = Z.
(IV.15)
The total constituent count is consequently u + d = 3A. This
follows from the three constituents per nucleon already present
in the slot deﬁnitions. It is a conservation check on the aggre-
gation, not an additional empirical prediction.
For the rest-source channel, every nucleon has the same con-
tribution:
Qmass = 4κZ +4κZ +4κ(N −Z)
= 4κ(Z +N) = 4Aκ.
(IV.16)
For coupling source, only the proton and excess-neutron slots
contribute:
Qsub = 8Zκ + N −Z
8
.
(IV.17)
Finally, adding the gap entries gives
∆Q = −4κZ +4κZ +(N −Z)7093
192
= (N −Z)7093
192 .
(IV.18)
Equations (IV.13)–(IV.18) are the six identities exported by the
nuclear ledger. Their exactness is conditional on the input vec-
tors. Once those vectors are accepted, no statistical ﬁt or nu-
merical tolerance is required to obtain them.
One can also subtract Equation (IV.17) from Equa-
tion (IV.16). The result agrees with Equation (IV.18), demon-
strating that aggregation commutes with taking the channel dif-
ference. This is a useful structural consistency: the gap can be
computed locally and summed, or the two full channels can be
summed and then subtracted. An implementation that yields
different answers has mixed slot meanings or numerical con-
ventions.
IV.4 Balanced cancellation and incremental structure
A proton and its balanced neutron have combined vector
ϕp +ϕnb = (3,3,0,8κ,8κ,0).
(IV.19)
The zero in the ﬁnal column is the central cancellation. It says
that the two channels agree for this paired contribution. It does
not say that the pair has zero physical binding energy or that
it is dynamically inert. Neither energy nor a transition rate
appears in the deﬁnition of the vector.
Adding the neutralizing electron gives (3,3,1,8κ,8κ,0). A
balanced system with N = Z consists of Z copies of this book-
keeping packet, and so its aggregate gap vanishes. This ex-
plains why a gap-based asymmetry term must vanish on the
balanced line. Other binding contributions need not vanish
there. A system can be balanced in this speciﬁc source sense
while still having surface, electromagnetic, and pairing struc-
ture in a subsequent readout.
Holding Z ﬁxed and adding one neutron in the admissible
domain changes the aggregate by precisely ϕne. Hence
∆Nu = 1,
∆Nd = 2,
∆Ne = 0,
∆NQmass = 4κ,
∆NQsub = 1
8.
(IV.20)
The increment in the gap is 7093/192. Here ∆N means a dis-
crete change in N, not the already deﬁned channel gap ∆Q.
20

SAM Volume II | Chapter IV
Brady | 10 November 2026
The notation makes explicit that an isotope-chain step has a
predictable ledger increment even when its energy increment
remains unknown.
A different operation adds one proton and one neutron to-
gether while preserving the neutron excess N − Z.
It con-
tributes one balanced packet and one electron. The gap re-
mains unchanged, but A grows by two and both source chan-
nels grow by 8κ. This distinction becomes important in the
next chapter: a normalized squared gap can change when its
denominator changes even if the gap itself is ﬁxed. Balanced
growth and excess growth are therefore distinct operations in
the same ﬁnite ledger.
IV.5 Worked ledger examples
Small examples expose what the general formulas actually
count. They are arithmetic illustrations, not new numerical
experiments or energy predictions.
For deuterium, Z = N = 1, and the population vector is
(1,1,0,1). The constituent counts are u = d = 3 and e = 1.
Both source channels equal 8κ, and the gap is zero. This ex-
ample shows the cancellation in its smallest neutral admissible
instance. The absence of a gap does not remove the measured
binding of deuterium; rather, it shows why the gap alone can-
not be identiﬁed with total binding.
For tritium, Z = 1 and N = 2, giving populations (1,1,1,1).
Its constituent counts are u = 4, d = 5, and e = 1. The two
channels are
Qmass = 12κ,
Qsub = 8κ + 1
8,
(IV.21)
and their difference is 7093/192.
Every slot appears once,
making tritium a particularly transparent demonstration of the
difference between constituent equality and source-channel
equality. It is also the exact equal-population exception in the
CR278 control history.
For helium-4, the populations are (2,2,0,2), the constituent
counts are (6,6,2), and the two source channels equal 16κ.
This is twice the balanced ledger packet. It is not a claim
that helium-4’s physical energy is twice deuterium’s: that in-
ference would apply linear source accounting to a nonlinear
bound-state observable.
For an iron-56 ledger example, Z = 26 and N = 30. The pop-
ulations are (26,26,4,26); the constituent counts are u = 82,
d = 86, and e = 26. The rest source is 224κ, while the cou-
pling source is 208κ + 1/2. Their difference is 7093/48. Un-
like an energy ﬁt, this calculation requires no measured atomic
mass. Its inputs are the isotope counts and the frozen slot as-
signments.
These examples also clarify the role of an isotope name. The
label is a convenient way to specify (Z,N) for a known isotope.
The algebra operates on the counts. It does not acquire addi-
tional predictive authority because a row happens to carry a
familiar name. Conversely, applying the same algebra to an
unobserved count pair produces a model ledger, not evidence
that the corresponding nucleus can be synthesized or will per-
sist.
IV.6 What equal-population symmetry means
At N = 2Z, the four population entries are all equal:
np = nnb = nne = ne = Z.
(IV.22)
A permutation of equal coefﬁcients leaves their weighted sum
unchanged. In particular, swapping the balanced and excess
neutron vectors between equal populations cannot change the
aggregate. The individual vectors are still different. The invari-
ance belongs to the population-weighted total at this speciﬁc
relation between N and Z.
The
technical
spine
records
that
CR278
retained
a
BOUNDARY artifact verdict because a precommitted wrong
control required a balanced/excess label swap to break every
N > Z row. It broke 55 of 56 such rows. Tritium, with N = 2Z,
was the exception.[2] This outcome can be understood exactly,
without changing the source verdict.
To see the mechanism, let the original neutron contribu-
tion be Zϕnb + (N − Z)ϕne.
The swapped contribution is
Zϕne +(N −Z)ϕnb. Subtracting the original gives
(2Z −N)(ϕne −ϕnb).
(IV.23)
Because the two neutron vectors differ only in their coupling
and gap entries,
ϕne −ϕnb =
�
0,0,0,0, 1
8,−1
8
�
.
(IV.24)
The swap therefore vanishes identically when N = 2Z. It also
leaves constituent counts and rest source unchanged for every
row, even away from that line. It tests the source assignment,
not the constituent inventory.
The correction is conceptual rather than numerical. A wrong
control cannot be required to distinguish transformations that
are exactly invariant on part of the admitted domain.
The
precommitted requirement nevertheless failed as written, and
the boundary verdict remains part of the record. Explaining
why a control was overstrong does not retroactively convert its
recorded outcome into an unqualiﬁed pass. Equally, the failed
universal requirement does not make the six aggregate identi-
ties ambiguous; those identities can be veriﬁed directly from
the deﬁnitions.
IV.7 Recovering counts and distinguishing information
content
The count channels provide a simple inverse consistency check.
From u = 2Z +N and d = Z +2N, one obtains
Z = 2u−d
3
,
N = 2d −u
3
.
(IV.25)
A valid integer ledger must recover nonnegative integral counts
and satisfy the chosen domain.
The electron column must
agree with the recovered Z for the neutral bookkeeping con-
vention. These tests are stronger than checking u + d = 3A
alone: many inconsistent count pairs can have the same sum.
The source channels also retain complementary information.
Rest source determines A through A = Qmass/(4κ), while the
21

SAM Volume II | Chapter IV
Brady | 10 November 2026
signed gap determines N − Z through Equation (IV.18). To-
gether these recover Z and N in the admitted domain. Thus
two separately typed channels encode both total size and im-
balance. A single measured number would not automatically
preserve this decomposition, especially after a nonlinear read-
out combines several effects.
This is the practical meaning of keeping the columns sepa-
rate. Compression into one scalar can be useful for a particular
comparison, but it discards the route by which that scalar was
constructed. A binding residual cannot be reverse-engineered
into unique slot assignments merely because the ledger itself
is invertible in (Z,N). The inversion applies to the stated exact
columns, not to a noisy energy measurement or to an arbitrary
ﬁtted combination of them.
IV.8 From an isotope row to an element family
An isotope row is indexed by (Z,N), with A determined by
their sum. An element-family row ﬁxes Z while permitting a
collection of neutron counts. A representative isotope is one
chosen member used to display or evaluate that family. These
are three different objects. A table with one row per element
does not become a complete isotope inventory merely because
each row has a neutron count attached.
At ﬁxed Z, changing N traces the source increments de-
scribed above. The four-slot construction supplies a uniform
way to write those increments, but it does not determine which
isotope should represent the family. A selector for abundance,
longevity, a historical benchmark, or a model frontier would
be additional information. That selector must remain visible
when a binding score is reported over an element table; oth-
erwise, row selection can be mistaken for a universal claim
across all isotopes.
The technical spine distinguishes the historical 162-row
scoped isotope roster from the 126-row native periodic surface
and the one-representative-per-element CR277 binding read-
out. Those counts answer different questions. They should not
be forced into agreement by adding or deleting rows from the
nuclear ledger. The next chapters use these distinct surfaces
where appropriate, preserving their selectors and limits.
IV.9 The exact handoff to binding
The ledger exports constituent counts, a total rest source, a
coupling source, and a signed imbalance. It does not export
a physical radius, a state vector, a force law, a half-life, or a
general binding energy. The absence of those outputs is not
an algebraic defect. It deﬁnes the boundary between the ﬁnite
construction and the physical readout problem.
A useful handoff is therefore explicit: construct the pop-
ulation vector, accumulate the two source channels, retain
their difference, and only then apply a speciﬁed readout. The
readout must name its geometry, coefﬁcients, unit conversion,
and comparison target.
If measured binding were allowed
to choose the native population assignment, construction and
evaluation would no longer be separated. If a linear sum of slot
debits were called the full energy law, the accounting identity
would be asked to do work it was never deﬁned to do.
The next chapter follows an exact consequence of these
channels: squaring the source gap and normalizing by rest
source yields (N − Z)2/A. This carries the two-channel struc-
ture into the nonlinear asymmetry readout. Its coefﬁcient re-
tains the adopted source assignments, and physical energy con-
version uses its own speciﬁed map.

SAM VOLUME II
Binding: exact structure, historical ﬁts and the retained held test
Chapter V
Binding is where an exact constituent ledger encounters a
harder question: how does the conﬁguration change the ob-
servable mass or energy of the combined system? The ledger
of Chapter IV answers which typed contributions have been as-
sembled. It does not make their energy additive. A nucleus can
share the same total nucleon count with another nucleus while
differing in charge, imbalance, parity, and conﬁguration. A
useful binding readout must respond to those differences with-
out letting the measured answer determine the construction it
is meant to test.
The record contains three distinct achievements. First, the
source-channel gap has an exact normalized-square identity.
Second, historical models use that structure within ﬁtted nu-
clear readouts and preserve both successful comparisons and
failures.
Third, the current B5T authority reports a target-
blind informational tensor assignment followed by a leakage-
controlled held-chain residual comparison.
These achieve-
ments are related, but none should be substituted for another.
The exact identity is not a complete energy formula; the ﬁt is
not a derivation of all its coefﬁcients; and the held comparison
is not a physical identiﬁcation of the tensor.
This chapter follows that sequence.
Its sources are the
frozen SAMA binding chapter SAMA-D000032, the techni-
cal spine’s historical synthesis, the inspected CR245 runner,
and the binding authority updated on 16 August 2026. That
frozen authority records the retained B5T benchmark.
The
later source, ﬁeld, atomic and common-energy constructions
are developed in the added chapters. Older phrases such as
“present applied closure” in the technical spine are read in their
historical setting rather than used to displace B5T.[1, 2, 7, 4]
V.1 Why a direct source-to-mass bridge was abandoned
The preceding mass analysis established the need to separate
native source support from measured mass. The nuclear ev-
idence makes the reason especially concrete.
CR239 tried
a direct bridge with one carbon-12 anchor.
The retained
manuscript reports a non-anchor RMS residual of 0.189480,
compared with 0.00202 for the locked mass-number control.
Its residual had Spearman correlation +0.984 with neutron ex-
cess N −Z.[1, 3]
An anchor ﬁxes a conversion or normalization at the anchor
point. It cannot establish that the same map carries the cor-
rect variation across other isotopes. The large structured resid-
ual was therefore more informative than the exact closure at
carbon-12. It indicated that the direct readout was missing
a dependence associated with neutron imbalance. The failed
result survives as a reason for channel separation rather than
being discarded after a later construction performs better.
CR240 supplies the corrected distinction between rest
source and coupling source.
CR241 retains an additional
warning about uniqueness: low holdout residuals can coexist
with multiple admissible coefﬁcient candidates. Its recorded
candidate-artifact failure is not repaired by referring only to its
small residual. The nuclear argument requires neither a unique
mass candidate nor a universal source-to-mass identity. It re-
quires the two typed channels to remain separate long enough
for their difference to be studied.
This is the conceptual starting point of binding in the present
volume. A source packet may be exact in its own units while
still needing a readout law. The statement that an energy com-
parison is downstream does not render that comparison arbi-
trary. It speciﬁes which quantities are constructed, which are
calibrated, and which are tested. Keeping those roles separate
makes a failed comparison capable of correcting the readout
without silently rewriting the input ledger.

The coefﬁcient is exact under the stated source assignments.
It is not independently measured in this derivation. Neither
the numerical closeness of a candidate energy coefﬁcient nor
a successful ﬁt changes the conditional character of this result:
different source assignments would generally produce a differ-
ent coefﬁcient.
The empty row A = 0 is excluded from normalization. Its
gap may vanish, but 0/0 does not deﬁne a normalized asymme-
try observable. The algebra can be formally extended to signed
N −Z, and its square is even under reversal of that sign. Such
an extension does not manufacture nonnegative excess popu-
lations for N < Z. The identity’s algebraic symmetry and the
population construction’s domain must therefore remain dis-
tinct.
V.3 What the asymmetry shape explains
Introduce the dimensionless imbalance fraction
I = N −Z
A
.
(V.7)
Then Equation (V.6) becomes CasymAI2. At ﬁxed total size,
the normalized-square quantity grows quadratically with im-
balance. At ﬁxed imbalance fraction, it grows linearly with
size. This is the useful structural content of the normaliza-
tion: the same expression distinguishes an extensive contribu-
tion from a relative composition variable.
At N = Z, the quantity is zero. If one adds balanced proton–
neutron packets while keeping the absolute excess ﬁxed, the
numerator stays ﬁxed and the denominator increases. If in-
stead one adds excess neutrons at ﬁxed Z, both numerator and
denominator change. These are different paths through the
ledger, so the normalized result is not simply a constant cost
per neutron. The nonlinearity is already present before an en-
ergy coefﬁcient is attached.
The square also removes the sign of the gap. That is ap-
propriate for an asymmetry magnitude, but it discards informa-
tion. The signed gap remains useful when tracing the original
source channels. A downstream calculation that retains only
its square cannot recover which side of a formally extended
balanced line generated it. This does not harm the stated iden-
tity; it explains why the squared channel should not replace the
complete ledger in storage or interpretation.
To use the identity in an energy readout, suppose a model
contains the term
−λ (∆Q)2
Qmass
.
(V.8)
In the conventional count basis, its coefﬁcient is then aasym =
λCasym. Equation (V.5) ﬁxes the conversion between two rep-
resentations of the same term. It does not ﬁx λ, the physical
energy unit, or the sign convention for the whole observable. If
λ is ﬁtted, the resulting energy coefﬁcient remains ﬁtted even
though the basis conversion is exact. This is the point at which
a conditional algebraic coefﬁcient and an empirical energy co-
efﬁcient must not be merged.
The source synthesis reports exact veriﬁcation of the identity
on 71 rows in CR245. Such a row check is a valuable imple-
mentation receipt: it shows that the program’s channels obey
the algebra on its data. It is not the reason the identity holds for
arbitrary admitted integers. The proof above supplies that rea-
son. Conversely, the proof cannot certify that a particular ﬁle
was parsed correctly or that every stored row used the intended
units; those are tasks for the implementation receipt.[2, 7]
V.4 Why binding needs more than the gap
The manuscript organizes the larger readout into volume, sur-
face, Coulomb, asymmetry, pairing, and family-gated compo-
nents. In schematic form,
Gbind = V +Snuc +C
+Aasym +Ppair +Gfamily.
(V.9)
The plus signs mean assembly of components; the signed con-
tributions are contained in their deﬁnitions. This is a map of
the needed dependencies, not a declaration that the full physi-
cal geometry has been derived.
The historical CR245 design matrix makes the distinction
explicit. Its ﬁve columns are

“Linear ﬁt” in coefﬁcient space therefore does not
mean “linear constituent binding” in physical space.
The terms serve different roles. The volume term scales with
total count; the surface term introduces a different size depen-
dence; the charge-dependent term distinguishes nuclei with dif-
ferent proton populations; the asymmetry term penalizes com-
position imbalance in the chosen sign convention; and the par-
ity term distinguishes even–even, odd–odd, and mixed-parity
rows. These interpretations explain why a single total-source
scalar cannot reproduce every column. They do not establish
the coefﬁcients from the substrate or determine shell-local de-
tails absent from this basis.
A simple sum of constituent debits or contact fees lacks
these distinct dependences unless they have already been in-
serted into the fees. The historical failure of such a sum is
therefore informative. It excludes a particular shortcut from
ledger to energy. It does not imply that contact structure is ir-
relevant; it says that counting contacts with the tested linear
rule did not close the binding curvature.

V.5 Reading the CR245 implementation literally
The inspected CR245 runner separates several stages that are
sometimes compressed into the phrase “binding from typed
substrate.”
Stage one uses exact rational arithmetic to con-
struct the channels and verify Equation (V.6). Stage two ﬁts
the ﬁve-column form to training rows with A ≥ 16. Stage three
searches a ﬁnite collection of typed-rational candidates near
the ﬁtted coefﬁcients. Stage four builds and evaluates the can-
didate described by the program as “zero-free.” The order mat-
ters: the candidate choice occurs after a ﬁt to measured data.[7]
The code also identiﬁes the numerical comparison target.
For each row it deﬁnes
Bcode
u
= A−matom,u,
(V.12)
where matom,u is the numerical atomic mass in uniﬁed atomic
mass units. It multiplies residuals in this proxy by the recorded
conversion 931.49410242 to express an MeV-equivalent scale.
Equation (V.12) is a deﬁcit relative to A atomic mass units. By
deﬁnition, it is not automatically the total energy required to
dissociate a nucleus into free nucleons. That latter quantity
uses a different reference state. This chapter therefore calls
Equation (V.12) the runner’s mass-defect proxy and preserves
the exact implemented target when describing its ﬁts.
This distinction is not cosmetic.
Changing the reference
mass changes the target and potentially the ﬁtted coefﬁcients.
A familiar-looking basis does not establish that two programs
ﬁtted the same observable. Historical reports using “binding”
must be read together with their actual target deﬁnition. No
new numerical conversion or reﬁt is performed here, and the
stated proxy should not be silently corrected in prose while
leaving the reported metrics untouched.
The coefﬁcient search draws its candidates from a speciﬁed
collection of inherited atoms, reciprocals, ratios, and special
combinations. A ﬁnite deterministic candidate list is a repro-
ducibility aid. However, selecting the candidate closest to an
empirical ﬁt is still data-informed selection. The inspected
function build_zero_free_coef replaces a ﬁtted coef-
ﬁcient if the nearest typed candidate is within ﬁve percent; oth-
erwise it retains the ﬁtted value. Thus the label “zero-free”
does not describe a universally parameter-free, preregistered
prediction.
In symbolic terms, if �β j is a ﬁtted coefﬁcient and tj its se-
lected typed candidate, the implementation uses

is a ﬁt-and-snap construction with ﬁtted fallback. Its output
can be evaluated honestly as a candidate model, including on
held rows. What it cannot establish merely by its name is that
all coefﬁcients were deduced before empirical information en-
tered.
The runner’s gates retain further useful speciﬁcity. It asks
for at least three of ﬁve coefﬁcients to have candidates within
ﬁve percent, and for the snapped candidate’s training and test
RMS to remain within a factor of 1.5 of the ﬁtted reference.
Those are tolerance-based comparisons with a ﬁtted model.
Passing them would show that the substitutions preserve a cho-
sen level of performance, not that the substituted constants
have been uniquely identiﬁed by physics. The technical spine
accordingly retains the exact asymmetry bridge while stating
that the broader candidate did not close the full binding prob-
lem.
V.6 What the historical controls establish
A control earns its meaning from the change it actually makes.
In CR245, dropping the asymmetry column and reﬁtting the
remaining four columns asks whether that column adds use-
ful structure in the selected training lane. Shufﬂing the target
labels and reﬁtting asks whether the original association be-
tween rows and values carries information. Neither control, by
itself, identiﬁes a microscopic mechanism. Their value lies in
excluding speciﬁc explanations of the ﬁt.
The runner also perturbs an R-dependent factor on the right-
hand side of the asymmetry identity while holding the con-
structed source channels ﬁxed. The control replaces the de-
nominator 192 · 7117 = 16 · 12 · 7117 by 16R′ · 7117 for se-
lected alternative R′. For a nonzero gap, the equality should
then break. This is a check on the frozen identity factor. It is
narrower than regenerating an alternative physical theory with
every R-dependent input changed consistently.
Balanced rows have N = Z, so both sides are zero for any
such factor. The code explicitly separates those trivial rows
from the nontrivial ones. This is the same logical discipline
illustrated by the equal-population exception in Chapter IV: a
control cannot distinguish a change on rows where the mea-
sured expression is invariant under that change. Recording the
invariant subset is part of interpreting the test, not a reason to
discard the entire control.
Together these controls establish bounded facts about the
chosen expression and ﬁt. The exact rational stage checks in-
ternal equality; term removal checks usefulness within a basis;
shufﬂing checks row-target association; and coefﬁcient pertur-
bation checks dependence on a frozen factor. Combining them
improves the evidence record, but does not collapse their dif-
ferent meanings into a universal proof of nuclear binding.

asymmetry, and pairing shapes within its stated geometry. Its

A separate connection-fee experiment tested the simpler lin-

current SAMA synthesis explicitly warns that no exact perma-

This history supplies a sequence of increasingly speciﬁc
